The Long-Wavelength Approximation and the Electric Dipole

Representation

Atoms and molecules are composed of charged particles (electrons and

nuclei) forming bound states whose size a_0 is typically on the order of a

few Bohr radii.

Assume that such a system interacts with radio frequency

infrared

visible

or ultraviolet radiation.

The wavelength λ of this radiation

is large compared with a_0

and it is legitimate to neglect the spatial

variations of the electromagnetic field over the size of the system of

particles

all the particles see the same field.

This is the long-wavelengthapproximation.

For the sake of simplicity

consider an atom (or a molecule) that is

globally neutral and located close to the origin O.

To lowest order in α, its

electrical properties are characterized by its electric dipole moment

d= Σ qr, (70)

A unitary transformation on the Hamiltonian (40) can cause the coupling

between the atom and the field to appear explicitly in the form of an

electric dipole interaction between the atomic dipole d and the radiation.

To higher orders in α/λ

the same procedure would result in the

appearance of electric quadrupole and magnetic dipole interactions

etc.

Because we neglect them here

we also omit in H the spin magnetic

coupling H_B

so that we start with the approximate Hamiltonian

H= Σ_{j=1}^N {p_j^2 / 2m_j + V_{oo}(r_j) + Σ_i ħω_i(a_i a_i^† + ½)} (71)

a) THE UNITARY TRANSFORMATION

The transformation

T= exp[- (i/ħ) d·A(0)] = exp[Σ_j Σ_i (q_j/ħ) a_i e_j A_i(0)] (72)

where

A_i(0) = Σ_s (ħ / 2ε_0ω_s V)^{1/2} (a_s e_s + a_s^† e_s)

is a translation concerning both the operators p_j and the operators a_s and

a_s^†.

The fundamental operators are in fact transformed according to the

following rules

Tr_j T^† = r_j (74.

T p_j T^† = p_j + q_j A(0) (74.

T a_s T^† = a_s + Σ_j (q_j/ħ) e_s·r_j (74.

T a_s^† T^† = a_s^† + Σ_j (q_j/ħ) e_s·r_j (74.

Because the transformation is time independent

the new Hamiltonian is

written

H' = THT^†

= Σ_j {p_j^2 / 2m_j + V_{oo}(r_j)} + Σ_i ħω_i(a_i^† a_i + ½) + H_dip

+ Σ_i {a_i Σ_j (q_j/2m_j) [p_j·A_i(0) - iħe_i·∇]} (75)

where

H_dip = - (1/2ε_0 V) Σ_{i

j} ω_i (d·e_i)(d·e_j) (76)

H_dip = - (1/2ε_0 V) Σ_i ω_i (e_i·d)^2.

b) THE PHYSICAL VARIABLES IN THE ELECTRIC DIPOLE REPRESENTATION

The physical variables in the new representation are represented by the

transforms G' of the observables G which represent them in the original

representation

G' = TGT^†.

(77)

Starting with expressions (37)

(21), (23)

and (22) for the operators

representing

respectively

the velocity of the particle a

the transverse

vector potential

the magnetic field and the transverse electric field

obtain expressions for the new observables which represent these vari-

ables:

v_j' = T v_j T^† = (p_j - q_j A(0)) / m_j (78)

A' (r) = TA (r)T^† = A (r)

= Σ_s (ħ / 2ε_0ω_s V)^{1/2} (a_s e_s e^{ik_s·r} + a_s^† e_s e^{-ik_s·r}) (79)

B'(r) = B(r) (80)

E'_{t}(r) = TE_{t}(r)T^†

= Σ_s (ħ / 2ε_0ω_s V)^{1/2} { i(a_s e_s e^{ik_s·r} - a_s^† e_s e^{-ik_s·r}) }

= E_t(r) - P_t(r)/ε_0 (81)

where P_t (r) is the transverse part of the polarization density P(r) associ-

ated with the atom when it is considered as a pointlike dipole

P(r) = d δ(r) (82)

Note that, according to (74.

a), r_j = r_j

so that the position of the particles

the atomic dipole and the polarization density are represented by the

same operators in the two representations.

c) THE DISPLACEMENT FIELD

Starting with the total electric field E(r) and the polarization density

P(r), we introduce the displacement field

D(r) = ε_0 E(r) + P(r).

(84)

We study its properties in reciprocal space

where it is written

D(k) = ε_0 E(k) + P(k) (85)

= ε_0 (E(k) - i k × A(k)).

[Relation (82) has been used to calculate the Fourier transform of P(r)].

With expression (39.

a) for p(k), the Maxwell equation (2.

a) is written:

iε_0 k × k × E(k) = Σ_j (i e^{−ik·r_j} / V) [k·p_j e_j - k·p_j e_j e^{ik·r_j}]

= Σ_j (e^{−ik·r_j} / V) [k·(k·p_j) r_j - k^2 p_j] (86)

The expansion in powers of kr_j

made in formula (86) is justified in the

long-wavelength approximation because kr_j = kq_j <1.

The first term

inside the brackets of formula (86) is zero because the system of charges is

assumed to be globally neutral.

The second term is expressed as a function

of the electric dipole.

We then have

in the dipole approximation

iε_0 k × k × E(k) = (1/V) k × k × d (87)

From (5) and (87)

it is clear that k ·E(k) is zero so that the displacement

field is transverse for a globally neutral system.

Moreover

the polarization P(r) is zero outside the system of charges

(r > a_j), and (84) is reduced to

E(r) = D(r)/ε_0 for |r| >a.

(88)

The displacement field is thus the transverse field which coincides

except

for the factor ε_0

with the total electric field outside the system of charges.

One advantage of the new representation is that a very simple operator

describes D(r)/ε_0

and thus the total electric field.

Indeed, according to

(84), the transversality of D results in the fact that D = ε_0 E + P.

As a

consequence of (81),

D_t(r) /ε_0 = E'_t (r) + P_t (r)/ε_0

= E_t (r)

= Σ_s (ħ / 2ε_0ω_s V)^{1/2} {i(a_s e_s e^{ik_s·r} - a_s^† e_s e^{-ik_s·r})} (89)

d) ELECTRIC DIPOLE HAMILTONIAN

We now return to the Hamiltonian H'.

The last term of (75) is the new

interaction Hamiltonian.

Using (89), it is written

H_dip = - d · D'(0) /ε_0, (90)

and represents the interaction energy between the electric dipole and the

displacement field.

It is frequently written in the form

H_dip = - d · E' (0) (91)

it being understood that E' (r) is the mathematical operator defined by

(29) or (89) as a function of a_s and a_s^†.

It is usually designated by the term

“electric field”

although it coincides with this variable only outside the

system of charges.

H' is thus written

H' = H_{rad} + H_{part} + H_dip.

(92)

H_{rad} is given by (45)

H_dip by (90)

and H_{part} by

H_{part} = H_{part}^0 + H_{dip}^{self}.

(93)

The new particle Hamiltonian is obtained simply by adding to H_{part}^0 given in

(50) a dipolar self-energy described by expression (76).

However, it should

be noted that

in H_{part}

p_j^2/2m_j

is indeed the kinetic energy of the particle

a, according to (78)

whereas this is not the case in (50)

because the

velocity is given by (37) in this representation.

In all the foregoing

the atom is assumed to be at rest at the origin of

the coordinate system.

In certain problems

it is important to take into

account the motion of the center of mass R of the atom.

If the atom is

globally neutral

it is sufficient

in the electric dipole representation

replace 0 by R in H_dip:

H_dip = - d · E' (R).

(94)

Atom-Photon Interactions

Basic Processes and Applications allows the reader to master

various aspects of the physics of the interaction between light and matter.

It is

devoted to the study of the interactions between photons and atoms in atomic and

molecular physics

quantum optics

and laser physics.

The elementary processes in

which photons are emitted

absorbed

scattered

or exchanged between atoms are

treated in detail and described using diagrammatic representation.

The book pre-

sents different theoretical approaches

including

Perturbative methods

The resolvent method

Use of the master equation

The Langevin equation

The optical Bloch equations

The dressed-atom approach

Each method is presented in a self-contained manner so that it may be studied inde-

pendently.

Many applications of these approaches to simple and important physical

phenomena are given to illustrate the potential and limitations of each method.

CLAUDE COHEN-TANNOUDJI is Professor of Physics at the College de

France.

He is the coauthor of Quantum Mechanics

published by Wiley.

Cohen-Tannoudji is a member of the French Academy of Sciences and a

Foreign Associate of the National Academy of Sciences in the United States.

He is a corecipient of the 1997 Nobel Prize in physics.

JACQUES DUPONT-ROC holds a full-time research position at CNRS

within the Laboratoire Kastler Brossel at the Ecole Normale Supérieure.

Dupont-Roc earned his PhD in atomic physics at the University of Paris.

GILBERT GRYNBERG is Professor of Physics at Ecole Polytechnique and

maintains a full-time research position at CNRS.

Dr. Grynberg earned his PhD

in atomic physics at the University of Paris.
